loved and will be forever in his family's hearts and minds.
Helen D. "Denise" Galloway, 65, June 10, 2019
For over 23 years, Denise worked for the Lunenburg Public Schools. She worked in the
library at Turkey Hill and was the administrative secretary at the former T.C. Passios and
Lunenburg Primary. To many, Denise was the first person parents spoke with when
enrolling their children in school. She will be remembered for her kind heart and the way
she could reassure even the most nervous new students. She greeted everyone with a
warm and welcoming smile. To all who knew and loved Denise, she will be deeply
missed.
Catherine "Catie" Keefer, 40 June 14, 2019
Catie along with her sister owned and operated a daycare in Lunenburg for several years.
She was a lifeguard and swim instructor at the Lunenburg Town Beach in the summer
months. She was also very active in Lunenburg Youth Soccer for many years as a coach
and a parent. Some of her favorite things were going to the ocean whenever possible, her
lime green Jeep Wrangler, annual trips to Disney World and Gettysburg,PA. The most
important people to her were her two sons, and her family.
Janice P. (Davies) Carrier ,75, September 9, 2012
Jan worked as the Administrative Assistant for the Conservation Commission. She retired
from the position that she loved in December 2015. Jan loved her community and
demonstrated that through her service to the same. Whether serving on building
committees, the Cemetery Commission, the Historical Society, the Caucus Committee or
any of several other groups, she always had something to give. She was a dedicated
member and former secretary of the Lunenburg Turkey Hills Family Lions Club. She
served as Lunenburg's Election Warden for over 45 years. and was usually the first face
you would see in the lobby upon entering to cast your ballot. Jan enjoyed spending time
out and about with friends and family. Her children and grandchildren meant the world to
her.
Patrick Michael Barney, 41, October 5, 2019
Patrick was appointed as a Reserve Officer at the Lunenburg Police Department
July 1, 2000 and became a full-time Officer on January 13, 2002. He was a Squad
Leader when he graduated from the Massachusetts Police Corps. He always hoped
that Lunenburg would initiate a canine force as he loved animals, especially his
dachshund, Sable. After a critical incident in 2006, Pat developed PTSD and as a
result retired from the Police Department on August 8, 2012. Patrick then lived
between Massachusetts and Florida. Patrick had a joyous smile, infectious laugh and
was always willing to help others. Even in his death, he donated his organs so that
others could enjoy life

FY' 19 TAX RATE-$18.68
OPEN TOWN MEETING GOVERNMENT- FIVE MEMBER BOARD OF SELECTMEN
ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY
ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
AREA- 26.63 SQUARE MILES
ROAD MILES- ABOUT 100
Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of
approximately 26.4 square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts
approximately 43 miles from Boston. It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and
the City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on
the east by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of Townsend.
Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting of all registered
voters in the town. Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeting, the affairs of
the town are generally administered by a five member Board of Selectmen and a Town
Manager. Local school affairs are administered by an elected seven member School
Committee while local taxes are assessed by a three member Board of Assessors, all are
appointed on an at-large basis for staggered three-year terms.
Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Three water
›odies account for somewhat of a clustered approach to the town's historical development
Lake Whalom and the former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District
from the remainder of Lunenburg. This district was developed as a housing base for many
factory workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster and Fitchburg. In many
ways the characteristics of the Whalom District more closely reflect characteristics of these
cities than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills area of town, around Hickory Hills
Lake, was developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. The
third body of water is Shirley Reservoir, a public water body also developed with mainly
vacation cottages that have now been modified to year-round residences. Commercial and
industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two major
arteries (Routes 2A and 13), and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of
Leominster's and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards
had slowly developed into single tamily housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg.

===PAGE 23===
and Board of Selectmen continue to work hard on serving the best interests of the Town and
providing a high level of services while remaining fiscally responsible to the taxpayers.
During 2019 there were many topics addressed and accomplishments to highlight:
• As part of the annual goal setting process, the Selectmen reviewed their goals from FY19
and formalized their goals for FY20. The goals the Board identified for FY2019 included
identifying, defining, discussing, and finalizing the criteria to be used in determining the
use and/or re-use of the Town Hall, Ritter, old Primary, Brooks House, and TC Passios and
to propose at least one project to the 2020 Annual Town Meeting regarding the use and/or
reuse of the old Primary School. The Board identified the criteria used in determining the
use and/or re-use of the aforementioned buildings in the fall of 2019 and discussed the
future use of the old Primary throughout the year and continued to narrow down the focus
for the future use of this building into 2020.
• The Town Manager's goals included developing and implementing steps towards the
redevelopment/reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, implementation of a recycling
contamination enforcement program, and planning/implementing accessibility
improvements.
• There were multiple projects that addressed accessibility at various town facilities, town
property, and public rights of way throughout the year. In January the Town Manager
announced receipt of an ADA Project Grant from MA Office on Disability for $7,500 for
accessibility improvements to the Lane, Cowdrey, and Small Town Forest properties. Also
in January the Veterans Service Office was relocated from the Eagle House to the Ritter
Memorial Building to be more accessible for veterans as the previous office was located on
a second floor without a handicap lift so the Veterans Officer was relocated to the Ritter
Memorial Building lower level that has a lift. The Town Manager coordinated the use of
Montachusett Technical students to renovate the second floor Town Hall restroom to
become a handicapped accessible bathroom and this was started in February and completed
by May. In March the Town Manager applied for and ultimately received a District Local
Technical Assistance grant to update the Town's ADA Self-Evaluation and Transition
Plan. The Town was one of three communities that participated in a Public Rights of Way
study through our regional planning agency that used a consultant to review the town's
sidewalks, ramps, and other ADA requirements. Around June the handicapped parking
spot at Town Hall was moved from an uneven brick sidewalk to a concrete level spot, the
chairlift door at Town Hall was fixed, and the uneven granite steps at the Ritter Memorial
Building were repaired.
21
===PAGE 24===
of the program, the town's contamination rate was cut by twenty percent.
• The redevelopment and/or reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, an old vacant gas station
with potential environmental hazards, has been a property the Town has sought to clean-
up for over a decade but due to the unknown environmental hazards have not taken the
property for back taxes so that the onus of the potential clean-up costs was not borne by
the Town. After much research and consultation with the State and legal counsel, we were
able to gain an Administrative Search Warrant that gave the Town the ability to apply for
grant funding to perform an environmental assessment and right to access the property for
the inspection and sampling needed. In September the Court granted the Administrative
Search Warrant and the Town Manager submitted a Brownfields Site Assessment Grant
through Montachusett Regional Planning Agency for the costs of the environmental site
assessment, which was approved. The environmental site assessment is expected to be
complete by the summer of 2020 and the project will be eligible for clean-up costs
through MassDevelopment.
• In the fall of 2019 the Town Manager worked with a consultant to apply for grant funding
through the Municipal Vulnerability Preparedness (MVP) Program, which provides
support for cities and towns to plan for climate change and implement key adaptation
actions for resiliency. The planning grant applied for is a necessary step in order to apply
for construction costs to implement projects that are identified during the planning phase.
In 2020, the Town was notified we received this grant.
• After successfully applying for District Local Technical Assistance from our regional
planning agency to create a business brochure and create a permitting guidebook, both
were completed in January. The Business Brochure was created with assistance from the
Lunenburg Business Association and Land Use Director in an effort to highlight the
town's natural resources and increase awareness of these locations to residents who many
not know where public access to trails and conservation lands are located. The Permitting
Guidebooks was created to assist customers with projects of any type and scale to simplify
how they can file permits. Another project that was applied for and received through our
regional planning agency was for a Walkability and Parking Study. This study focused on
traffic data, crash data, an inventory of bike and pedestrian infrastructure for the Main
Street area and Whalom Lakefront area. The report was finalized in November and
identified a number of recommendations to address improving the walkability and parking
in both the Main Street and Whalom area.
22
===PAGE 25===
was held with different stakeholders that would be affected by decisions relative to the
future of the TC Passios, old Primary Building, Ritter Memorial, Town Hall, and the
Brooks House. On January 15" the Selectmen had a joint meeting with the Architectural
Preservation District Commission, Historical Commission, and Historical Society to
discuss their concerns regarding the condition of the old Primary School and future of the
building. At the conclusion of the meeting, Chairman Jamie Toale stated the goal of the
Board was to ask town meeting for funding for detailed plans for the TC Passios so that an
actual cost to move town offices there would be known. In February the Board met with
the Conservation Commission, Open Space, and the Parks Commission to discuss the plan
to use TC Passios for municipal office space, school central offices, and community
space. In March the Board met with the Public Access Cable Committee and town
employees to hear their input on consolidating town offices at the TC Passios building. In
May the Board met with another group of stakeholders including the Council on Aging
members, COA Director, Library Trustees, Library Director, and Teen Center personnel.
In September the Board met with the Architectural Preservation District Committee to
discuss the old Primary building and the Brooks House, in terms of future use and how
this aligned with the regulations under the APDC Bylaw. In November the Board
conducted a survey of potential uses for the old Primary School that was handed out at the
Special Town Meeting and the town meeting also included a non-binding vote on the
demolition of the old Primary School. The Board continues to discuss this goal and move
forward on a potential re-use.
• On January 22"d our legislators, Senator Tran, Representative Benson and Representative
Hay came before the board to announce the $130,000 state earmark for abatement of
asbestos at the Turkey Hill Elementary School.
• In March the Town partnered with the solar company Nexamp to promote their
community solar program to Lunenburg residents for discounted electricity. The program
was so successful it reached its cap and had a waiting list. Later in the year, Nexamp
secured another solar location that enabled those on the waiting list to take advantage of
these discounts.
• In March the Selectmen created the Land Acquisition Committee to examine and
negotiate the purchase of land to be used for conservation and recreation purposes at 994
Northfield Road. Ultimately, the Committee was successful in negotiating a price of
$775,000, which was below the appraised value and secured $500,000 in grant funds
towards the purchase of land to be used for conservation and recreations purposes at 994
Northfield Road. Town meeting approved the purchase of land at the November 2019
Special Town Meeting.

===PAGE 26===
included the hiring of an additional full-time fatrolman, addressed the need tor increased
Overtime costs for the Fire Department, a part-time Recreation Director, and Assistant
Town Manager/HR Director. The Annual Town Meeting was held on Saturday, May 4* and
approved a total budget for FY20 of $38,058, 164.30.
• This year was another incredible year for road projects in Lunenburg. Besides the
continuation of the $4M Pavement Management Plan on local roads, the Town was the
recipient of a Small Bridge Grant that was originally applied for in June of 2017 in the
amount of $379,000 to reconstruct the Pleasant Street Bridge over Pearl Hill Brook. The
project requires design work phases that must be approved by MassDOT as part of the
process. The project is expected to be complete by the spring of 2022. The DPW also
undertook culvert repairs on Northfield Road and Sunset Lane and repairs to the Townsend
Harbor Bridge. The Summer Street Road Reconstruction project that was awarded in 2018
was substantially complete by the fall of 2019.
•
The Town had begun the process of becoming a Dementia Friendly community in the latter
half of 2018 and had a kick-off event in June of 2019. The Dementia Friendly Action group
had a lot of success in a short amount of time and the initiatives they undertook were
modeled by other communities.
• As part of the Town Manager's Goals for FY19, the Town Manager completed an
Employee Handbook and Procurement Manual. The Employee Handbook codified the
town's hiring process, personnel policies and practices and the Procurement Manual
provided written procedures for town departments for proper procurement of supplies,
services, and construction projects. The Board adopted a Reimbursement Policy.
The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY
2020.
• In July, as part of an annual review of the Selectmen's Policies and Procedures, the Town
Manager presented various updates to the Board's Manual including a revised Recycling
Policy, Public Comment Policy, Chapter 61 Policy, among other minor revisions to other
policies. The Board also adopted a new Social Media policy.
• In October the Town Manager, the School Superintendent, and the Land Use Director held a
Symposium to discuss the historical growth patterns and projected future growth/
development and how this would impact Town and School services and needs. Some of the
patterns that were evident from the data was there would be a steady growth in the gaining
population in Lunenburg, a rise in racial and ethnic diversity, the need for increased staffing
and the need for additional space for added staff.

===PAGE 27===
the agreement to $139,000.
• The Special Town Meeting in November included a number of articles such as amending
the FY 2019 budget to capture additional tax growth, transferred available funds into the
Special Purpose Stabilization account, the acquisition of property on Northfield Road for
conservation and recreation purposes, by-laws changes, and two Home Rule petitions for
additional liquor licenses.
In December, the Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler,
weekday entertainment, automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses.
Throughout the year, the Board reviewed Earth Removal permits, liquor license transfers,
Special Event permit requests, flammable storage licenses, pole petitions, tollbooth
requests, and held Dangerous Dog hearings.
As of writing this report, it has been three and a half years since I was appointed your Town
Manager and it is a responsibility that I take very seriously. The residents of Lunenburg
demonstrate the true meaning of community that I have witnessed in various acts, whether it
be supporting our local businesses, non-profit groups raising funds for those in need, or
words of gratitude for our public safety and public works departments. I have the privilege
of working with many talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents and
business owners of Lunenburg and are consistently looking for ways to better serve the
public. The accomplishments noted above, among the numerous day-to-day responsibilities,
could not happen without the cooperation of dedicated staff, appointed, and elected officials
that make the wheels of town government turn. Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine
Peterson and Assistant Town Manager Julie Belliveau for all your hard work and keeping the
office running smoothly. I would also like to thank the committee and board members,
department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to the residents of town.
Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the department heads that lead our various
departments: James Marino, Police Chief; Patrick Sullivan, Fire Chief; Jack Rodriquenz,
DPW Director; Karen Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance Director; Myleen Mallari,

our town run as efficiently as possible. Lastly, thank you to the residents and businesses that

Clerk- Cullen Dwyer
Member- Brian Corcoran
In 2019, the APDC inspected the Old Primary School and pursued ongoing efforts to preserve the structure.
Through various meetings and letters, the Town Manager and Select Board were told that the building was in a
process of Demolition by Neglect, that it was their responsibility to maintain the building, and that through
inaction the town was in violation of the APD bylaw. Outside funding was sought to arrest the deterioration.
Negotiations with the Select Board raised the question of how buildings were determined to be in Demolition by
Neglect, and if notices of violation were applied evenly. To address the issue, Vice-Chair La Veck drafted a
procedure to review the complete inventory of historic buildings in the APD on a semi-annul basis.
Neglect of the Brooks House was also a concern. By town meeting vote, the Town Manager was authorized to
sell the structure and have it moved to some other location. As this resolution was contrary to the APDC's
mission, various town entities were contacted to begin crafting an alternate solution that would preserve the
building in its present location.
LaVeck had noted a type setting error when the APD bylaw was submitted to the State Attorney General's
office for ratification.
The error omitted certain portions of the bylaw's text, including the definition of Demolition by Neglect. The
Select Board assured the APDC that the error would not affect the application of the bylaw, while the Town
Manager endeavored to correct the error.
Chairman McGrath led the effort to design and order new road signs to mark the APD, which will be installed
iran cran bed the e
The APDC reviewed and approved several Certificates to Alter, including:
136 Leominster Road- replacement of siding and trim
876 Massachusetts Avenue-re-roof of barn
92 Leominster Road- re-roof of house
909 Massachusetts Avenue (Harley House)- renovation of porch and certain windows and doors.
An administrative review was also conducted for Dawn Marshall at 42 Main Street (Captain Jones House) for
the construction of an apartment in the barn.
Cullen Dwyer, Clerk
Lunenburg APDC

===PAGE 60===
Officer. Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Building Department,
as well as the Zoning Board of Appeals Administrative Assistant. Jack Biery serves as the
Wiring Inspector. Gary Williams serves as the Plumbing Inspector and Gas Inspector. The
purpose of the Building Department is to ensure State regulations and Codes are being met as
well as local bylaws. This includes new construction, alterations, additions, remodels and
annual inspections of certain public use groups such as restaurants, churches, bars, daycares,
meeting halls, schools, and others.
Building Permits for fiscal year 2019 are as follows:
120 gas permits
122 plumbing permits
248 electrical permits
461 building permits, including driveway, mechanical, and fire suppression permits
951 total permits issued
The state of Massachusetts continues to follow a group of International Codes amended by
each state. These codes cover a variety of building elements from mechanical systems, energy
conservation, primary building practices, installation of fire suppression systems, and others.
Currently we are in the 9 edition of 780 CMR, the Massachusetts State Building Code.
In addition to the Building Codes and Zoning Bylaws, the Building Department also assists
the Town Clerk with Business Certificates. This is achieved by way of a "Form of Intent".
This form is used to ensure local bylaws are met by the applicant. Once the form is completed
by the Building Department, a Business Certificate can be applied for at the Town Clerk's
office.
All applications for new work requiring permits are available online through the Town
website. The Building Department continues to use ViewPoint online permitting. Paper
applications are available at the Building Department office as well.

meetings for a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family upkeep
projects and larger scale industrial/commercial proposals along the Electric Avenue section of Lunenburg. The
Conservation Commission worked to spell out goals of preparing to use the year 2020 to work on clarilying the
land use policy to provide consistency with the original bylaw and to find those sections that may be now out of
date and need to be modified. This effort is estimated to be completed by summer of 2020. The Conservation
Commission completed revisions to the local wetland regulations which are now in effect and were presented to
the Town Clerk for posting
The Commission also held numerous inspections with the Commission Administrator on weekends prior to
meetings to familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The
Commission continues to work with the Town on familiarizing itself with the upcoming storm water mandates
from the EPA and working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary codification as well as
aiding with the community Notice of Intent to the EPA under the "MS4" regulations.
The Conservation Commission implemented its forest stewardship planning after receiving grants continuing the
efforts outlined in last year's annual report. These stewardship plans, adding the Hollis Road property, is oriented
for enhancing bird wildlife habitat in conjunction with the Mass Audubon Society as part of the DCR "Forestry
for the Birds"
and will aid the Commission in implementing environmental management plans which include
timber stand management. The Commission retained Gary Gouldrup of New England Forestry Consultants, a
noted forester with many years of experience working in Lunenburg and all of the adjacent towns/cities in this
region serving those communities and private landowners. The Conservation Commission also held public
recreational walks through its open space parcels and plans to continue to do so. The Commission has, and
continues to hold public forums on the implementation of its open space policies and timber-stand management.
As a result of some of the meetings and forums held, the Commission continues to apply for and receive funding
from the Commonwealth to author stewardship plans for our Conservation Areas.
Copies of the current environmental stewardship plans are available on the Conservation Commission website at:
http://www.lunenburgma.gov/content/204/222/default.aspx
This past year, the Commission successfully completed the collection of a single $11,000 fine for wetland
violations on one single property. Aside from the collection of the fine, the Commission ordered and oversaw the
successful remediation of the wetland resource areas being restored to pre-alteration condition.
The Commission has also completed the successful implementation of the installation of handicap parking and
handicap accessible trails at the Small Town Forest, Cowdrey and Lane Properties. The funding was provided by
a grant from the Mass Office on Disability.
The Commission has also continued to work closely with Lake Shirley and Hickory Hills in the implementation
of their management plans becoming actively involved in continuation of formulating updates designed to give a
better picture of the overall health of the ecosystems and the various ways to ensure continued protection of these
valuable resources.
The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and looks forward to many more!

The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
supportive services, programs and education. Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts General
Law Chapter 40, section 8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or services and to
develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues.
The FY19 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative Assistant
and Transportation; Pauline Roy, Outreach Coordinator; Sandra Ricci, Meal Site Coordinator; Judy Alario and
Elsa Watson, Meal Site Assistants; Jim McGuigan, Paul Grunditz, and James Barrett, MART Van Drivers.
The Council on Aging Board is an eleven member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Lunenburg Board of
Selectmen. The FY19 Council on Aging Board members were; Chairperson Pete Lincoln, Vice-Chairperson
Jacquelyn Dwyer, Betty DiGiacomo, Sarah Grant, Brian Guenard, Deb Lincoln, Kevin McNally, Cheryl Moisan,
Diane Nowd, Jane Rabbitt, and Judith Tarbell.
The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House
Supporters Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, and
Montachusett Regional Transit Authority.
We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle House. In FY 19 we had 57 volunteers who
contributed 2,452.75 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the meals on wheels
program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Café, office help, library, and friendly visitors. We greatly
appreciate their service.
The Eagle House offers a variety of programs, such as health and wellness, arts and crafts, intergenerational,
bocce, shuffle board, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, Memory Café and social events both at
the senior center and offsite.
There were approximately 978 people who utilized the senior center in FY19. Those 978 people compiled 28,764
units of duplicated service by participating in the programs listed above.
In FY19 the Council on Aging delivered 4,319 Meals on Wheels to 46 clients and provided 2,964 rides to 71
clients on our senior van.
We are very fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance of
supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our elders.
Special thanks go to our 501c3 fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc. This group provides funding for
new living room furniture, office/kitchen supplies, various programs & entertainment, garden upkeep and spring
mulch at the Eagle House Senior Community Center. Also special thanks to all of our outside agencies for
donating time and programming to our clients.
The Lunenburg Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you online at
www.ourseniorcenter.com or you can pick up a copy at the Eagle House Senior Community Center, Town Hall,
Library, Post Office, local churches and various businesses throughout the community.

===PAGE 65===
VAJ
"The mission of the Department of Public Works is to protect, preserve, and improve the Town's
infrastructure and related assets in a manner which meets and exceeds the current and future social
and economic needs of the community, to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality environment for
the Town's residents and visitors, and to provide a cost effective and environmentally sound means
of disposing of solid waste and wastewater:"
DEPARTMENT PHILOSOPHY
Honesty and integrity are absolutes. They characterize the only forum in which anything
worthwhile can be accomplished.
Efficiency: Make every effort to proceed correctly the first time. It may take a bit longer but
will not require repeated attempts.
Loyalty up and down the chain of command is essential to success.
Every employee is essential. Those at the top are support people. The real work is done by
the men and women who drive the trucks, operate the equipment, turn the wrenches, and provide a
seamless flow of paperwork.
DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
Public Works staff is either working or on call twenty-four hours a day, three hundred and
sixty-five days a year, prepared to respond to any weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility
related emergency that may arise.
We strive to enhance the awareness of the value Public Works provides to our Town, and
ensure others are aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to
make sure efforts are not dismissed as they merge into the fabric of our everyday expectation of
service.
Major events, particularly in the form of devastating storms, over the past years have
highlighted the fact that Public Works has a seat at the table and is a major player in emergency
management as first responders. Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is
critical in order to facilitate the required services of others. We must continue to build on this
awareness.
This department must reach out to the public we serve, continuing to make them aware of
the fact that the common services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a
dedicated staff of individuals, service companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control,
solid waste collection and recycling operations, tree removal, storm water management, grounds
maintenance, winter snow and ice removal, cemeteries and parks are necessary services supporting
everyone in the community.
We will continue to engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather
support for annual budget requests, capital requests, and specific projects.
63
===PAGE 66===
Increase involvement with our insurance provider to make better use of available resources in an
ettort to obtain credits toward lessening premiums. Safety is our first concern
• Set realistic expectations. Make clear through public education that due to limited resources,
particularly in the area of personnel, the level of service provided by Public Works is not always
optimal.
• Work diligently to establish and maintain a high level of courteous communication and interaction
between members of all boards and commission.
• Establish a comprehensive evaluation of bridges and culverts, and prioritize by condition, use, and
value a plan for rehabilitation
PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
It had become a high priority goal of the Board of Selectmen as Road Commissioners, to
achieve a pavement condition of 72%-75% town-wide. While state-supported Chapter 90 funds remain
tagnant, the dedicated Pavement Management line in the operational budget has grown substantially
Additionally, with the infusion of funds facilitated by a four million dollar debt exclusion supported b
residents, the Comprehensive Pavement Management Plan has been hugely successful providing
smooth and safe transportation for our residents and all those travelling through our community.
The debt exclusion funding combined with state and operational funds has nearly tripled the
total paving value of years past as we attack a nine million dollar backlog of repairs.
Time spent addressing potholes and other roadway deficiencies diminish exponentially as new
paving increases. A clear benefit of the dollars expended. There is much work yet to do but expectations
are high.
The Summer Street project burst into full development after many years of aggressive lobbying
by this department, Town ofticials and members of our legislative branch. As of this writing, the ten
million dollar rehabilitation of sub-surface utilities and total reconstruction of the roadway from
Leominster to John Fitch Highway in Fitchburg is complete. This represents the single largest roadway
project in the history of the Town of Lunenburg.
In addition, funding has been procured for the reconstruction of the Pleasant St. Bridge through
the State's Small Bridge program. We have also been awarded a grant to enhance the "walkability" in
town center by way of a successful application to the Complete Streets Design Committee and the
Office of the Secretary of Transportation.
Acquiring supplemental funding allows the completion of projects that would otherwise not be
viable due to the extraordinary expense.
FLEET MAINTENANCE
The DPW Maintenance Division is responsible for the repair and preventative maintenance to all Town
vehicles and equipment. High performance police units, first responder Fire and Rescue apparatus, park,
cemetery and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway-related machinery
64
===PAGE 67===
As Director, I have developed and submitted to Town Officials a vehicle replacement schedule based
upon that created by the American Public Works Association in order to more clearly represent our
capital needs.
FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:
DPW Facility 520 Chase Road
Public Safety Complex
Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.
Town Hall
Town Beach
Town Common
Ritter Bldg.
Teen Center
Old Primary School
Veterans Park
The Eagle House
All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding the school campus.
A facilities staff of one individual remains on-call 24 hours a day 365 days per year.
Collectively we attempt to keep buildings compliant with current codes to insure a safe, secure work
environment. Any significant interruption of service within Town buildings carries the potential of
disrupting or disabling necessary services provided to the community. The Facilities Director is a
problem solver and technician with a wide range of experience encompassing all of the construction
trades. Together with a stable of reliable professionals, we deliver services directly or oversee those
functions provided by specialized subcontractors. Unfortunately, the creation of a capital replacement
program and a definitive plan for all our buildings has fallen short of expectations. Years of neglect
have created a significant backlog of necessary repairs and improvements and the process has become
reactionary at best. The Building Reuse Committee reported clearly that asset management relative to
our buildings requires a professionally prepared assessment and a long-range capital plan. To this end,
our plan for 2021 is to continue with the contracted services of a Project Management Consultant to
survey, quantify, project building needs and establish an annual cost. Additionally, we will revisit the
Vertex Report and identify "envelope" tasks that if funded, would protect the structures from further
exterior failure. Public Works continues its use of a building maintenance software program as a
framework for day-to-day activities. Requests for service are acknowledged immediately, prioritized,
and completed in a timely manner either in-house or by sub-contracted services. Those individuals
requesting service are notified upon completion of the tasks and a history of the repair is filed
electronically.
Until we as a town can achieve consensus in the form of a Town-wide plan, we will make little
progress. For example, years have passed and still there is no clear path as to the future of the old
Primary School. As caretakers it is difficult to create a definitive approach given the uncertainty of
future use.
65
===PAGE 68===
to avoid paving over problems. In the future new Federal and State storm water regulations will likely
force the Town to become more proactive albeit with no additional identified source of funding.
At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall, drainage structures in close proximity to
open water are cleaned and inspected. Public Works maintains an aggressive schedule of street
sweeping and catch basin cleaning, the front line of storm water management.
Waste Water
The DP W continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system. Day to day maintenance of the
overall wastewater system inclusive of 10 pump stations is outsourced to a specialty vendor but
continues to be the responsibility of Public Works. Business management, inspections, and permitting,
remain in-house and the responsibility of the DPW.
Tree Removal
Storm damage together with the natural attrition creates a backlog of tree felling and pruning
that far exceeds the allotted budget. The Department is unable to fulfill each resident request and must
prioritize work based upon the calculated degree of danger to residents and property. With the
establishment of a Hazardous Tree Survey, a more efficient approach to defects has been established
through prioritization. Sadly, the amount of work far exceeds the available revenue. Until there is a
substantial infusion of funds, the DPW will act in a reactionary mode. All work aside from brush
removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees is outsourced due to the necessity of a bucket
truck or crane and a licensed professional. Each spring DPW staff initiates the activity of removing and
disposing of woody debris within the right-of-way together with a town-wide program of curbside
mowing. An aggressive program by our energy provider in recent years has lessened the burden of the
Town to remove those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community.
Landfill
The "capped" landfill off Young's Road remains in compliance with the inspection protocol
mandated by the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive
methane gas as well as groundwater contamination.
The frequency of the testing, reduced through a special permit as a result of consistently
favorable results, remained constant throughout the year. The landfill site including the perimeter
fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
determined by the EPA to be hazardous waste. We are allowed to store these materials through the
procurement ot a (BUD) Beneticial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires yearly testing of
these materials. Other communities must transport these tainted materials to an appropriate site at a
substantial cost to the community.
On weekends during the fall and spring, DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual
composting of brush, leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and
our overall recycling program.
66
===PAGE 69===
trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside collection of trash and recyclables by our current vendor,
Casella Inc. Pay as you throw... Recently we entered into an agreement allowing our "trash bag"
supplier to coordinate the effort between supplier and vendor resulting in some mixed reviews. DPW
remains the point of contact between residents and the vendor responsible for curbside pick-up.
Lake Shirley Dam
In the early 1990s with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding, the Lake Shirley Association turned
over ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for
maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed by a small number of volunteers from the
Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the
establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the
umbrella of the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP
mandates compliance with current and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and
support of a professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection
resulted in a positive report and we remain in total compliance.
Winter Operations
Snow and ice present a major challenge. The safety of the public depends on the ability of the DPW to
naintain our roadways at a high level of safety despite the severity or the duration of snow and icıng
events. To that end, we have developed a winter operations procedure based upon a detailed plan with a
flexibility to accommodate the unpredictability of each storm. The expectation of immediate service is
high and must be delivered to every part of town in a timely manner.
Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the capital plan.
Financial support is necessary to provide effective fleet rotation insuring minimal interruption of
services due to breakdowns and repairs. In the fall, salt is delivered to the Chase Rd. facility.
Lunenburg is in a consortium with 14 other communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible
price. From late summer to early fall vehicle maintenance staff inspects and prepares the trucks,
spreaders and plows for winter operations. During the construction season road crews address right-of-
way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects in order to eliminate obstacles to snow
removal. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter operations. Typically, DPW staff
combines with up to 14 subcontractors to provide service.
Generally, it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event, that determines the
overall cost. For example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than a
long duration freezing rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically, the
lepartment operates in a deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average winter.
It is a dedicated group of individuals who successfully challenge the worst Nature has to offer
regardless of severity or duration.
"DPW workers are the original Storm Troopers"

CEMETERY
One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North
and South cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with
the three members of the Cemetery Commission. The DPW supplies equipment and support staff
for, on average, forty burials a year and general maintenance of the facilities as required by Town
Charter.

changes in population and funding. In November, the Town Manager provided a target budget to
department heads as a guide to develop their budget requests. After individual discussions with the
department heads and listening to public comment during the budget presentations, the Town Manager
developed a final recommended budget. Hard questions were thoughtfully talked through in pursuit of
finding balance between ideal service and what can be funded at projected levels of taxes and new
growth. The fiscal year 2020 budget recommendation is for a 3% increase over FY19. The budget
would fund targeted additions in staff in response to the increased need for Town services resulting from
increased population. It funds the capital plan and the Town's stabilization accounts. It meets all the
financial policy requirements as set forth per Town Policy. The budget does not call for increases in
debt; the Town would continue to pay down its debt through FY 2020 and beyond. We are pleased to
report for the upcoming year Lunenburg remains fiscally healthy.

Lunenburg's total revenues are projected to be $43,133,920. The projection brings in an
additional $1,508,020 above what was collected last year. The estimated revenue breakdown

The tax levy is projected to increase by $904,568: the standard 2.5% increase over last year's tax base,

although it still has not reached the robust levels seen in past years. The Town benefits from non-
municipal revenues (e.g. grants, support groups, and donations). For example, the Library, Council on
Aging, and Fire Department have received funding for operating and capital expenses in the past year
from sources other than local taxes and state aid, thus keeping those expenses out of the budget. We are
grateful for the efforts that all departments put into finding outside sources of funding to help the Town
continue to move in a positive direction.
Expenses

accounts and OPEB ($450,000), and other non-operating expenses ($185,000). The total overall budget
increases $1,508,020, or a 3.6% increase.

The budget includes a capital plan which funds 10 projects recommended by the Capital Planning
Committee. Some of the larger initiatives are the Engine 4 replacement ($685,000), Turkey Hill
Middle School Asbestos Abatement/Ceiling replacement ($162,000), Fuel Tank replacement at DPW
($220,000), and the Rescue 2 replacement ($310,000). Smaller improvements include painting the
Teen Center exterior ($12,000) and the Wallis Park basketball court and fence ($36,000). The entire
capital plan for this year, $1,684,100, would be funded in this budget without taking on new debt. Note
that three capital improvements totaling $1,119,000, from the Vertex study, related to building repairs
for Ritter, TC Passios, and Brooks House are not funded by this budget.. In addition to operating
expenses and capital projects the Town is funding line items that serve to provide a cushion for the
Town's General Stabilization Fund ($100,000, per Town's Financial Policy) and the Special Purpose
Stabilization Fund for future large project capital needs ($200,000), as well as continuing to increase
the Other Post-Employment Benefits (OPEB) fund ($150,000.
Conclusion -The Town of Lunenburg is in the fortunate position of being able to expand services and
improve assets throughout while maintaining financial stability and security. This is reflected in the
Town's continuing AA+ bond rating. However, a recent Debt and Liability assessment found that
Lunenburg is in a weak debt position. This underscores the importance of not taking on new debt with
the Fiscal 2020 budget and will be an important factor when looking ahead.

mount a bronze plaque, bearing testimony to the site and the lives of those there buried
Commissioner and professional mason Richard McGrath coordinated with the Historical Society and
prepared new granite bases on which to reset the monuments. They have yet to be erected as the
search continues for the exact location of the burial plot.
The 18th century Battles House at 68 Elm Street was slated for demolition to make way for the
development of new homes. The Historical Commission invoked the demolition delay bylaw to seek
alternatives. By some combination of pressure from the Commission, cooperation of the property
owner, and good fortune, the house was sold intact with its 19th century barn to a new owner who
apparently intends to restore or renovate the old house.
Several of the road signs marking the Lunenburg Historic District had gone missing over the
years and plans were laid to order new signs to be installed by the DPW.
The weathervane that once sat atop Town Hall was missing for some time. Commissioner Bill Lakso
researched the design of the old weathervane and commissioned a new one to be constructed. The new
weathervane had yet to be installed at the close of the year.
With some regrets, the Commission approved the total demolition of the historic house at 822 New
West Townsend Road. The owner had previously intended to restore the building but after stripping
the interior discovered that decay and previous alterations had made the structure worthless.
Recognizing the owner's predicament, the Commission approved the demolition application without
Lunenburg continues to own historical buildings in the town center. The old Primary School and to a
lesser extent, the Brooks House, are falling into disrepair. The Town has no immediate municipal use
for these buildings, but voters at Town Meeting have been reluctant to sell them off. Seeing the
buildings languish in limbo was a major motivation for Chair Lantry to begin planning for the
adoption of the Community Preservation Act, which would generate funds for preservation and other
community projects.

giving more." I see evidence of that every day in my colleagues at the library. It is a joy for all of us to
work at the library and to see the burgeoning activity here: more than 80,000 items borrowed this past
year, almost 70,000 visitors, and more than 8,000 people attending more than 400 programs this past
year. Most afternoons you'll find dozens of people doing homework, playing board or video games,
researching, using the computers, and even hanging out-at the library!
I hope you're not surprised that the library is about more than just books. Residents come to the library
just to see a smiling face; community members and groups have meetings at the library; families come
to story-time; a father and son eat dinner weekly in our cafe for time out of the house; teens and adults
volunteer and gain job-like experience; jobseekers come for help on their job search; entire classes of
fifth graders come to learn to use the library; people come for help answering a question, to borrow our
telescope or electricity use monitor or ukulele; they use ebooks or audiobooks from home or their car;
they attend an exercise program or listen to a lecture in the community room; some even watch library
programs on the local access cable channel; and of course, many people come to see a movie or
borrow and discuss a book.
There are new faces on staff at your library. Retirements and graduations mean that five people
working at the library are new to their jobs in 2019-we only employ ten total-and the staff is happier
than ever to serve our community. The infusion of youth in our staff combines perfectly with several
experienced, long-tenured workers with vast amounts of institutional knowledge and dedication. As we
move forward into this new era, we are focusing in particular on collaboration and communication. We
collaborate amongst ourselves as a staff, we collaborate with other community organizations, and we
collaborate with other town departments, all to better serve the community. We also recognize that one
of our biggest opportunities is to better communicate to our community all of the services we offer.
When someone in town has a challenge, we want them to first ask themselves, "Can the library help
me with that?"
Our facilities also continue to be renewed. Our building is now more than 15 years old, a fact that
surprises many people who enter it for the first time, and to retain that like-new character we must
continue to renew it. We have to thank the generosity of the Friends of Lunenburg Public Library for
their role in keeping the library in its gem-like condition. They contribute more than $30,000 each year
to library goals, including programming, maintenance, collections, and supplies. Without the support
of the Friends, the library would be in a much more challenging position.
Growing from the successful 2018 renovation of the teen room—one of the key contributing factors
that has led to an explosion of teens spending their weekday afternoons in the library-we've focused
on creating a space for "tweens" as well. Coupled with a partnership with Turkey Hill Elementary
School to bring fifth grade students to the library to learn to use our resources, we've seen an
expanding number of library users from the 10-12 year old demographic this past year, too, especially
in response to programs targeted specifically at this group.

to continue to expand the number and variety of programs we offer our adult community. Between

We continue to experiment with new programming ideas as we seek to keep the library innovative and

Children's Services
Our community members have already discovered that the Lunenburg Public Library, in addition to
being a valued part of our community, is an integral part of early childhood development. Attendance
at weekly story times for children ages birth-5 remains strong. Our afterschool offerings for school-
aged children include LPL Kids Book Club, which occurs monthly and the children are enjoying the
lively book discussions, and related snacks. Enrollment in this group has increased 50% over last year!
We also offer monthly Lego Club Challenges that are well attended by both girls and boys and
encourages child/parent interaction and offers a STEM component.
Similarly, our monthly science, technology, engineering, art and math (STEAM) program also targets
elementary aged children to encourage and excite these young learners. A strong knowledge base in
these core subjects is one of the keys to a successful future, and this focus echoes school curriculum.
Our community outreach and support continue to benefit many area schools and groups. In addition to
visits from the fifth graders of Turkey Hill Elementary School, we provide weekly story times for an
active group of toddlers and families who are part of School Linked Services, preschoolers from the
Early Learning Center and various local family day care groups.
Thanks to a gift from the endowment of the Friends of Lunenburg Public Library, we now have two
early literacy computers with touch screen technology. Featuring games, storytelling programs, and
other resources, they are an excellent resource for early learners to develop pre-reading and literacy
skills.

Teen Services
In FY19, the library offered 190 teen programs which were attended by 1,687 teens. During the summer
133 teens read 1,677 hours! Summer program participation ran strong with 255 tweens and teens
attending 12 programs. One third of the summer programs were designed with Social Emotional
Learning (SEL) objectives in mind. We found success with these programs, and will apply this
framework to our future teen programming to improve its effectiveness.
2019 saw the introduction of new programs and the expansion of existing traditions. Every summer the
library partners with the 4H Worcester Extension Office to provide a babysitting training class for
tweens ages 11 and up, and in September, the library responded to a community need with a Babysitting
Connection Program so that parents could meet with certified teen sitters to find the best match.
This year's new "Volunteen"
program is a community service-based program offering teens the
opportunity to earn community service hours, engaging in supportive activities, like creating cards for
the Pink Revolution Breast Cancer Alliance Initiative and stitching for a cause with the library's
Stitchcraft group.
The Dungeons and Dragons Club is another highlight, as it continues to grow and now has two distinct
campaigns run by different groups. Sharing leadership responsibilities and rotating Dungeon Masters
creates learning opportunities and skills-building and allows them to explore storytelling elements.
Improvements to the usability of the collection will continue with a diversity audit, evaluating the
collection for inclusivity and representation, noting areas for improvement. This will generate data to be
utilized in future collection development.

Public Access Cable Committee (PACC)
Steve Walker, Chair
Faith Beall, Vice Chair

The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to
manage the Public Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the
town's only cable-service provider.
The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-
broadcast programming from both town functions and meetings, as well as programming from publicly
available sources.
Programming
Channel 9 - Public
Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 63% of the local programming on Channel 9 in FY19, up 6%
from 57% in FY18. The increase in local programming was largely due to additional videographers.
Local programs included high school sports, band concerts and school events. The remaining 37% of
programming was obtained through other media sources (Access Share) and online downloads
(MassAccess and PEMedia).

The strategy for the PAC studio in the upcoming fiscal years includes acquiring new studio space as
soon as municipal space becomes available. A new studio would provide a more permanent and
comfortable environment for producing programming.
Upgrades in FY19 included the addition of 3 new SONY cameras and tripods for videographers and for
loan. A large-scale monitor in the Town Hall Bilotta room was added for better presentation viewing by
the audience. New office chairs in the control room and studio were purchased. The Town Hall audio
system was adjusted for better performance during meetings with less feedback.
The community is encouraged to borrow PAC equipment or submit their own video for Channel 9
content.
Equipment upgrades accounted for 12% of the FY19 expenses (purchased under Line Item
"Equipment/Maintenance and Supplies). "
Staff, Producers and Volunteers
In FY19, there was one full-time Manager and 7 part-time videographers, classified as Vendors (Budget
Line Item: "Purchase of Services"). The Manager continues to administer the day-to-day operation of
the organization, including all production and programming schedules, staff scheduling, equipment and
services procurement, studio and inventory administration, advertising, and social media. Vendors
(videographers) provided video recording of committee meetings and additional video assignments, as
A technical contract consultant works with the PAC manager to provided technical and training
assistance as needed. It is expected that he will remain working for PAC through FY20. However, PAC
will be searching for a permanent part-time Production Technician to add to the staff by FY21.
Of the 63% of locally produced shows on Ch 9, most were produced by public access staff. Our resident
top producer, Pete Lincoln of "Talkin' Baseball," continued to create new programs for airing on Ch 9
through FY19. Other new programs include Knight's Roundtable and Community News. St. Boniface
continued to produce a weekly Mass program, on-air now for over three years.
Much effort has gone into recruiting volunteers, with little response. Social media and newspapers have
been used to promote volunteerism with little effect. PAC will continue to look for other ways to reach
out to the community for volunteers.
Staff (Salaries, Health Insurance, Purchase of Services (videographers), Consultant) accounted for
79% of the FY19 expenses.

2019 Committee Members:
Robert Hamel, Chairperson; Susan Doherty, Vice-Chairperson; Sheila Craigen, Clerk; Nancy Forest,
Myleen Mallari
The Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an
opportunity to have an abatement applied to their property tax bill. Participants worked in various
town departments to earn the tax abatement doing general office, custodial, library shelving, Meals
on Wheels, and kitchen assistance duties.
From January 1, 2019 to June 30, 2019 participants received $10 for each hour worked to earn the
$1,000 abatement. At this time income eligibility for participants was $40,000 for a one-person
household and $60,000 for a two-person household.
The Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off committee voted at the March 27, 2019 meeting to increase
income eligibility for participants of this program to $50,000 for one person households and $75,000
for two-person households.
Also voted at that meeting was an increase in the hourly wage from $10 to $12 per hour along with
an increase to the abatement cap up to $1,500. These increases became effective at the beginning of
FY20 (July 1, 2019).
There were seven Lunenburg residents who participated from January 1, 2019 - November 1, 2019.
These individuals as a whole, accumulated 837.25 hours which amounted to $8,761.00 in abatements.
Eligibility Requirements:
* Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
*Own and occupy a home in Lunenburg as their primary residence for at least one year
*Provide proof of income by submitting prior year income tax return information
*Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse's name who is also 60 years of age or older
* Meet the income guidelines of: one person household - $50,000 / two person household - $75,000
*All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check and must re- apply and be
accepted annually for participation in the program.
Earnings are exempt from state taxes only. Earnings are subject to federal and FICA tax
withholdings and are also subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either
Worcester Regional Retirement or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement.
Departments that participated between January 1, 2019 - November 1, 2019
*Lunenburg Board of Health
*Lunenburg Building Department/Zoning
*Lunenburg Council on Aging
*Lunenburg DPW/Sewer Departments
*Lunenburg Public Library

Joe for his service and leadership as Chairman.
The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning
for the future and developing and implementing rules, regulations and policies. Under the Town's
the operational side of the system to the Department of Public Works Director Jack Rodriquenz. The
Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 78% of Lunenburg
sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the other 22% is discharged to the City of
Fitchburg.
The Sewer Commission has contracted with Small Water Systems Services (SWSS) for the
operations and management of the ten pumping stations,
infrastructure since 2013. In FY19, we completed the chemergency generators and sewer
year with SWSS as our contractor for the
supervision of the town's sewer system. The Commission developed a scope of services for a new
O&M contract and put it out for bid in April 2019. Small Water Services (SWSS) won the bid and
the Commission began negotiations with SWSS for the new contract effective 7/1/19.
There are currently 879 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 352 which have
access to Town Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY19, 24 new accounts connected to Town
Sewer. The Commission billed out $835,329.23 in quarterly sewer bills, and had $44,556 of prior
year delinquencies added to the owners' property tax bills for non-payment. Five properties were
assessed a privilege fee in FY19. Two properties assessed were residential. Two assessments were
for 24-unit apartment buildings and one other property was a 3-unit condominium building.
Privilege fees help to pay down the debt owed by the Town on the original sewer project.
Throughout FY19, the Commission continued to work with several different groups regarding their
interest in sewer extensions, specifically at Lancaster Avenue, Upland Avenue and Chase Road. The
Commission is always willing to discuss a potential sewer extension.
In FY19, the Commission worked on updates to the Grease Trap Regulations, Connection Charges
Policy, Irrigation Meter Policy and Single Meter Policy. On October 30 and November 27, 2018,
the Sewer Commission held public hearings to inform residents of the proposed changes. The
Commission voted to accept the updated policies on November 27, 2019. For the new Irrigation
Meter policy, the Commission investigated options for new updated wireless irrigation meters and
worked with the Lunenburg Water District to select the new meter which can be read remotely by
the Water District on their regular rounds. With the updated Irrigation Meter policy that was
effective on August 1, 2019, irrigation meter customers will be charged an annual fee of $50 for
the cost to administer the program. The new policy will require residents with deduct meters to
convert their unit to one compatible with the Neptune remote reader by July of 2020.
The Sewer Commission developed a scope of work for a maintenance plan for the sewer system.
The contract was awarded to Wright Pierce and began in FY19. Wright Pierce will conduct an
evaluation of the equipment at all pump stations, working with Small Water Systems to develop a
written Maintenance Plan to insure the smooth operation of the system. The final plan will be
presented in FY20.
84
===PAGE 87===
worked with multiple establishments in correcting their non-compliance. The "Rules & Regulations for
Oil & Grease Traps/Interceptors for Food Establishments" continues to be a focus of the Commission as
Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance costs of the
sewer system. The Commission developed a FOG Audit inspection checklist and made plans to begin
audits of food establishments. While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public
disposal, individual homeowners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system
which increases everyone's costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.
In March 2019, the Commission voted to authorize a contract with Northern Data for bill print
outsourcing to streamline the billing process, which has continually grown as more customers have been
added to the sewer system. The Commission also voted to join the town's Solar Credit Program in FY 19
and anticipates saving on energy costs for running the pump stations in FY20.
At the May 2019 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward three articles which related to the FY20
budget and stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY20, $675,427.86 was appropriated
from Sewer Enterprise revenues and the sum of $163,251.14 was appropriated from Retained Earnings
for a total appropriation of $ 838,679.00 to operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY20. The amount of
$888, 706.40 was approved to cover both the Enterprise Fund's share of Town services and debt service
on the sewer betterments or the total approved cost of $1,727,385.40. The second article transferred
monies to the Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund in the amount of $27,521, approximately 3% of
proposed revenues as the Commission had done for FY18. This fund is to be used to maintain and
improve the operating efficiency of the system or cover large emergency expenditures. A main goal of
the Sewer Commission continues to be maintaining a balanced budget every year without having to
raise rates for our customers. This is the sixth year that the operating budget has been balanced without
a rate increase to the users. The third warrant article was to transfer the sum of $656.00 into the OPEB
Sewer Trust Fund. All three articles passed at Town Meeting. It was determined in FY 19 that any
monies collected for the Sewer Bank (I/I fees) or Reserve Capacity Stabilization Funds would be
transferred at the Special Town Meeting following the close of the fiscal year. A portion of the
connection charges collected at the time of an application for a permit to connect goes into these
accounts and targets resolving I/l issues and future capacity costs paid to the cities of Fitchburg and
Leominster for increased capacities.
The Commission continued working in FY 19 with Adam Costa (Town Counsel of Mead, Talerman
and Costa for guidance in contract negotiations and clarification of regulations. The Commission would
also like to thank Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director, for the seamless and smooth operation of the Sewer
Division of the DPW. They would also like to acknowledge and thank the Lunenburg Water District for
their partnership and good working relationship with the Sewer Division in providing valuable
assistance with meter readings, ownership changes, and status updates.

The Board of Health oversees the Septic System Repair Program which helps low to moderate income
families replace failed septic systems or connect to public sewer. Anyone interested in finding out
more about this program can contact the Board of Health office.
Homeland Security and Emergency Preparedness planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between
our agents, other Town departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg
but with our partner towns in our region.
This year we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new
wells, repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits. The Board continues its vigilance with
regard to septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its
general mandate to protect the environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.
The Board of Health provides other numerous services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the
Nashoba Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, numerous clinics are
provided throughout the year to our residents. These clinics provide inoculations, including flu shots,
blood pressure screenings, Well Oldster Clinics and dental screening. Fluoride treatment and dental
checks of our youngsters in the School System are provided by the Nashoba dental staff.
The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project. Mosquito
Control monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as
EEE and West Nile Virus and sprays frequently to control the population during the warmer times of
the year. Residents who want to have their property sprayed can do so by emailing to:
cmmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-3055, completing a request on their webpage at: http://
www.cmmcp.org, or calling the Board of Health office. This service is free of charge to residents.
With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to residents recovering
from illness and hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.
Please remember that our board members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the
citizens of Lunenburg.
If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the Board
of Health office at 582-4146 ext. 430.

FIRE DEPARTMENT
The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are
dedicated to providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to
the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
In 2019 your Fire Department responded to 1885 calls for service, broken down as follows:
Fires & Hazardous Conditions
420
Emergency Medical
949
Inspections
554
Other
26
Current Fire Department staffing is now 7 Career and 40-45 On Call Firefighters and
Emergency Medical Technicians, plus 1 full-time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator. The
station is manned 7 days per week with 2 Firefighter/Emergency Medical Technicians, plus a 3rd
Firefighter/EMT Monday-Friday from 8 am-4 pm.
This coverage is a combination of full-time
personnel in conjunction with 88 hours per week of per diem coverage. The per diem coverage is
provided by our Call Firefighter/EMTs staffing the station at their hourly rate. Staffing the station with
dual trained Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency occurs.
Though we have achieved 24 hour coverage in the station, many EMS calls and nearly all fire
calls still require more personnel than are on duty in the station. This means we still rely heavily on
the dedication and commitment of our Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians. Our call
personnel remain and will remain for the foreseeable future, a vital part of our emergency response. A
new Call Firefighter/EMT must attend over 340 hours of initial training plus additional time in
orientation and field training. Members qualifying to drive fire apparatus must complete an additional
65 hours of classroom and practical training. On average, it takes a new member one and a half to two
years to fully complete this training.
The department continues to move towards the goal of providing our own Paramedic Level
Ambulance Service. Though we currently operate two ambulances at the Advanced EMT Level, we
must still rely on outside agencies to provide paramedic level care when needed. Firefighter Timothy
Downey completed his paramedic training at the end of 2019 and is currently going through the state
testing process. Firefighter Austin Flagg also began the training in September and will be completing
it at the end of 2020. These two members, plus addition of three more firefighter/paramedics over the
next 2-3 years will allow us to provide our own paramedic level care to the community.
In 2019, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 554 Inspections.
This is in addition to
By law we are required to inspect smoke and
oll burner and propane gas installations. Any business holding a liquor license is also required to be
inspected for fire safety issues. In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans
reviewed for proper fire protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process.
94
===PAGE 97===
Trailer.
• Educational programs in conjunction with the Council on Aging. These included cooking and
heating safety, a CPR Demonstration and Ice safety.
• Training to certify personnel as Public Life Safety Educators. In 2019, Firefighter Austin Flagg
joined Captain James Ricci and Lt. Christos Lekaditis as the Department's Life Safety Educators.
→ Participation in the Senior Center's "Sand for Seniors" Program. This program provides buckets of
walkway sand for senior citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months.
In partnership with this program, Captain James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents and when
lacking detectors, Capt. Rice is able to install fire detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE
Grant.
→ It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent
smoke detectors.
During 2019, Call Firefighters Andrew Bolton and Olivia Hayes graduated from the
Massachusetts Firefighting Academy's Call/Volunteer Training Program.
This intensive 4-month
program provides the basic skills firefighters need to operate in today's challenging environments. All
newly hired Firefighters are required to complete this program within one year of hire.
Seven firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical
Rescue and Dive Teams. These regional teams respond to high risk and unusual rescue situations, such
as Confined Space,
Rescue from Heights, Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater
Underwater Search and Recovery. In conjunction with these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water
Rescue Unit and a Collapse/Trench Rescue Trailer.
Apparatus replacement continues to move forward. The new Rescue 2, a 2019 Ford F550/
Horton Ambulance was received in late 2019 and is now in service as our primary ambulance. Rescue
1 has been rotated to Backup/2nd Ambulance. The replacement for our 31-year-old ladder truck
experienced a delay in construction and is now due to arrive in April of 2020. This unit is expected to
serve the town for 25+ years. The replacement for Engine 4 has been ordered and is due to arrive in the
summer of 2020. This unit is expected to serve the town for 20+ years. The next anticipated major
apparatus purchases are not until 2026 (Rescue 1) and 2028 (Squad 3)
In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their
outstanding commitment to the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to
impress me every day. I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards and commissions
for their continuing help. Most people do not realize the level of interdepartmental cooperation that
occurs in the daily course of town business. My thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for
their ongoing support of public safety. It is that support that allows us to continue providing the high
level of service you have come to expect from us.
Patrick A. Sullivan, Fire Chief
